Representations of the quasi-partition algebras
Rosa Orellana, Nancy Wallace, Mike Zabrocki

Abstract
. The quasi-partition algebras were introduced in [ DO ] as centralizers of the symmetric group. In this article, we give a more general definition of these algebras and give a construction of their simple modules. In addition, we introduce two new algebras, we give linear bases and show that for specializations of their parameters, these new algebras are isomorphic to centralizer algebras. We provide a generalized Bratteli diagram that illustrates how the representation theory of the three algebras discussed in this paper are related. Moreover, we give combinatorial formulas for the dimensions of the simple modules of these algebras.



Dedicated to the memory of Georgia Benkart

1.
Introduction

The partition algebra was defined independently in the work of
Martin and his coauthors
[
M
,
M2
,
M3
,
MS
]
and Jones
[
J3
]
in the early 1990’s
as a natural extension of centralizer algebras such as the Brauer and Temperley-Lieb
algebras. It is of interest for combinatorial representation theory because
it provides a dual approach to resolving some of the open combinatorial problems
related to the representation theory of the symmetric group.

The partition algebras are indexed by an integer
k\geq 0
;
however in
[
M3
]
Martin introduced an intermediate algebra
that was recognized to lie between each integer indexed partition algebra. Halverson and Ram named them

half-partition algebras
(indexing them by
k+\frac{1}{2}
for each

k\geq 0
). They used the inclusions and Jones’ basic construction to construct the matrix units, and the irreducible representations of the algebras.

The partition algebras
\mathsf{P}_{k}(n)

are the centralizer algebras of the symmetric groups
S_{n}

when they act on the vector space
V_{n}^{\otimes k}

where
V_{n}\cong\mathbb{C}\mathrm{-Span}\{v_{1},v_{2},\ldots,v_{n}\}
.
One way of viewing this vector space is to notice
[
CST
, Section 7.5.5]

that as an
S_{n}
module,
V_{n}^{\otimes k}
can be realized as the
following iteration of induction and restrictions:

V_{n}^{\otimes k}\cong({\text{Ind}}^{S_{n}}_{S_{n-1}}{\text{Res}}^{S_{n}}_{S_{n-1}})^{k}{\mathbb{S}^{(n)}},

where
{\mathbb{S}}^{(n)}
is the trivial representation of the
symmetric group
S_{n}
. The half-partition algebras

\mathsf{P}_{k+\frac{1}{2}}(n)

fill in the chain of algebras as the centralizer
of the symmetric group
S_{n-1}
acting
on
{\text{Res}}^{S_{n}}_{S_{n-1}}V_{n}^{\otimes k}
.

The partition algebra shows promise for applications in
developing a better understanding of the representation theory
of the symmetric groups.
Indeed, Bowman-De Vischer and the first author
[
BDO
, Equation (3.13)]

identified the see-saw pair,
V_{n}^{\otimes k+\ell}
S_{n}
S_{n}\times S_{n}
\mathsf{P}_{k}(n)\otimes\mathsf{P}_{\ell}(n)
\mathsf{P}_{k+\ell}(n)

,

and used it to show that for sufficiently large
n
,
the reduced Kronecker coefficients
[
BOR
,
Mur
,
OZ2
]

are the multiplicities of
an standard
\mathsf{P}_{k}(n)\otimes\mathsf{P}_{\ell}(n)
module in the restriction of an irreducible
\mathsf{P}_{k+\ell}(n)
module.
They used that relationship to develop formulae relating
the reduced Kronecker coefficients, Littlewood-Richardson coefficients
and the usual Kronecker coefficients.

This approach seems like a difficult yet promising way to gain a better
combinatorial understanding of the reduced Kronecker coefficients.
Bowman, De Visher and Enyang
[
BDE
]
used this connection and bases
for irreducible representations of the partition algebras to
develop combinatorial interpretations for certain classes of
the reduced Kronecker coefficients using the structure of the tower
of algebras.

One source of the complexity of this approach could be that
the space
V_{n}
is not irreducible as an
S_{n}
module since

V_{n}\cong{\mathbb{S}}^{(n-1,1)}\oplus{\mathbb{S}}^{(n)}
.
Daugherty and the first author
[
DO
]
introduced a subalgebra of the partition algebra
that is related to this picture by instead of being the centralizer of the action of the
symmetric group on
V_{n}^{\otimes k}
, is the centralizer of
the symmetric group when it acts on
(\mathbb{S}^{(n-1,1)})^{\otimes k}
.
They showed that the quasi-partition algebra
\mathsf{QP}_{k}(n)

can also be realized as a projection of the partition algebra by an idempotent.

We note that there are other diagram algebras that are projections by
idempotents that can be found in the literature. A recent paper by
Doty and Giaquinto
[
DG
]
defines balanced elements of a planar
diagram algebra by conjugating by an idempotent of the partial Temperley-Lieb algebra.
The techniques we use to analyze the quasi-partition algebra
can apply to other algebras.

In this paper we develop the representation theory of the quasi-partition
algebras using a construction similar to the inclusion of the half-partition
algebras. Let
\mathrm{proj}
be the projection from
V_{n}
to the

S_{n}
irreducible representation
\mathbb{S}^{(n-1,1)}
. Then we remark
that

(\mathbb{S}^{(n-1,1)})^{\otimes k}\cong(\mathrm{proj}\circ{\text{Ind}}^{S_{n}}_{S_{n-1}}{\text{Res}}^{S_{n}}_{S_{n-1}})^{k}{\mathbb{S}^{(n)}}~.

Now in a similar construction to the partition algebra and the
half-partition algebra we have that there are two intermediate algebras
between the centralizer of
(\mathbb{S}^{(n-1,1)})^{\otimes k}
,
namely the centralizers of

{\text{Res}}^{S_{n}}_{S_{n-1}}(\mathbb{S}^{(n-1,1)})^{\otimes k}\hbox{ and }{\text{Ind}}^{S_{n}}_{S_{n-1}}{\text{Res}}^{S_{n}}_{S_{n-1}}(\mathbb{S}^{(n-1,1)})^{\otimes k}\cong(\mathbb{S}^{(n-1,1)})^{\otimes k}\otimes V_{n}

which we name
\mathsf{QP}_{k+\frac{1}{2}}(n)
and
\widetilde{\mathsf{QP}}_{k+1}(n)
.
This allows us to develop a Bratteli-like
diagram (see Figure
2
) to describe the relationship between the irreducible representations.
We say that this is ‘Bratteli-like’ rather than exactly a Bratteli diagram
because the edges between the
irreducibles of
\widetilde{\mathsf{QP}}_{k+1}(n)
and the irreducibles of

\mathsf{QP}_{k+1}(n)
represent a projection rather than an inclusion.

In analogy with the observations in
[
BDO
]
, we remark that there
is a similar see-saw pair,
\left(\mathbb{S}^{(n-1,1)}\right)^{\otimes k+\ell}
S_{n}
S_{n}\times S_{n}
\mathsf{QP}_{k}(n)\otimes\mathsf{QP}_{\ell}(n)
\mathsf{QP}_{k+\ell}(n)

.

Hence we have in analogy that the reduced Kronecker coefficients are the multiplicities of
an irreducible
\mathsf{QP}_{k}(n)\otimes\mathsf{QP}_{\ell}(n)
module in the restriction of
an irreducible
\mathsf{QP}_{k+\ell}(n)
module.

This paper develops the
quasi-partition algebras both as centralizer algebras (Theorems
6.1
,

6.2
and
6.3
)
and as projections of the partition algebra conjugated
by an idempotent (Equation (
4.6
)).
The main results are the construction of a tower of
quasi-partition algebras (Subsection
4.2
) and an explicit description of
bases of the irreducible representations of
\mathsf{QP}_{k}(n)

(Section
5
). The tower of algebras is used to
relate the dimensions of the irreducibles in the tower
using the inclusions and projections (Equations (
7.1
), (
7.2
) and (
7.4
)).
This allows us to give a combinatorial interpretation of the dimensions of
the irreducibles (Corollary
7.4
) and formulae for the dimensions in terms of
the numbers of set partitions and numbers of standard tableaux (Equations (
7.6
) and (
7.8
)).

Section
2
of this paper begins with a review of the
partition algebras, half-partition algebras,
and in Section
3
, we give a construction of their
irreducibles in terms of the standard modules. Although the
standard modules for the partition algebras were constructed and expressed in terms of
bases indexed by set valued tableaux in
[
HJ
]
, they do not include the standard modules
of the half-partition algebras and so we give a description of those modules
in Subsection
3.2
.
In Section
4
, we present three families of quasi-partition algebras,
and use them to build a tower of algebras.
In Section
5
, we construct the irreducible representations of
quasi-partition algebras.
In Section
6
, we show that the partition algebras that we defined
as projections of the partition algebra are isomorphic to centralizers
when the parameter is specialized to values of
n
that are sufficiently large.
Finally, in Section
7
we use the tower of algebras to give formulae for the
dimensions of the irreducible representations.

2.
The partition algebras

The partition algebra was originally defined by Martin in
[
M
]
. All the results in this section are due to Martin and his collaborators, see
[
M2
]
and references therein. For a nice survey on the partition algebra see
[
HR
]
.

2.1.
Definitions

For
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
,
x\in\mathbb{C}
, we let
\mathsf{P}_{k}(x)

denote the complex vector space with bases given by all set partitions of

[k]\cup[\overline{k}]:=\{1,2,\ldots,k,\overline{1},\overline{2},\ldots,\overline{k}\}.

A part of a set partition is called a
block
.
For a given block
B
, the set
B\cap[\overline{k}]

denotes the subset of all barred elements of
B
are referred to as the
bottom
of
B

and the set
B\cap[k]
denotes the subset of all unbarred elements of
B
and is referred to as the
top
of
B
.
Notice that for a given set partition
d
on
2k
elements,
then
d\cap[k]
and
d\cap[\overline{k}]
are set partitions on
k
elements.
We will let
\widehat{\mathsf{P}}_{k}
denote the set of all set partitions
of
\{1,2,\ldots,k,\overline{1},\overline{2},\ldots,\overline{k}\}.

Blocks with a single element will either be referred to as
singletons
or an

isolated vertex
. Blocks containing at least one element from
[k]

and one element from
[\overline{k}]
will be called
propagating blocks
; all other blocks will
be called
non-propagating blocks
.

For example,

d=\{\{1,2,4,\overline{2},\overline{5}\},\{3\},\{5,6,7,\overline{3},\overline{4},\overline{6},\overline{7}\},\{8,\overline{8}\},\{\overline{1}\}\},

is a set partition (for
k=8
) with 5 blocks. The block
B=\{1,2,4,\overline{2},\overline{5}\}
is propagating. The block
\{3\}
is a singleton.

A set partition in
\widehat{\mathsf{P}}_{k}
can be represented
by a
partition diagram

consisting of a frame with
k
distinguished points
on the top and bottom boundaries, which we call vertices.
We number the top vertices from left to right by
1,2,\ldots,k
and the bottom vertices similarly by
\overline{1},\overline{2},\ldots,\overline{k}
. We create a graph with connected components corresponding to the blocks of the set partition such that there is a path of edges between two vertices
if they belong to the same block. Note that such a diagram is not uniquely defined,
two diagrams representing the set partition
d
above are given in Figure
1
. Hence, a partition diagram is an equivalence class of graphs, where the equivalence is given by having the same connected components. In addition, we often omit the numbering on the vertices in the interest of keeping the diagrams less cluttered.

We will use the word
diagram
to refer to any element of
\widehat{\mathsf{P}}_{k}

or equivalently its partition diagram.

Figure 1.
Two representative graphs of the set partition
d
.

We define an internal product,
d_{1}\cdot d_{2}
, of two diagrams
d_{1}

and
d_{2}
using the concatenation of
d_{1}
above
d_{2}
, where we identify
the bottom vertices of
d_{1}
with the top vertices of
d_{2}
.
If there are
m
connected components consisting only of middle vertices, then

d_{1}\cdot d_{2}=x^{m}d_{3}

where
d_{3}
is the diagram with the middle vertices components removed.

Example 2.1
.

For example, consider the two diagrams

d_{1},d_{2}\in\mathsf{P}_{6}(x)
where

d_{1}=

\{\{1,3,\overline{4}\},

\{2,\overline{1}\},\{4,5,6,\overline{5}\},\{\overline{2},\overline{3}\},\{\overline{6}\}\}

and
d_{2}=\{\{1\},\{2,3\},\{4,\overline{1},\overline{2},\overline{4}\},\{5,\overline{6}\},\{6\},\{\overline{3},\overline{5}\}\}
.

Extending this by linearity defines a multiplication on
\mathsf{P}_{k}(x)
.
With this product,
\mathsf{P}_{k}(x)
becomes an associative algebra with unit of dimension
B(2k)
,
the
Bell number
which enumerates the number of set partitions of a set with
2k
elements.

Given diagrams
d_{1}\in\widehat{\mathsf{P}}_{k_{1}}
and
d_{2}\in\widehat{\mathsf{P}}_{k_{2}}
, we denote by
d_{1}\otimes d_{2}

the diagram in
\widehat{\mathsf{P}}_{k_{1}+k_{2}}
obtained by placing
d_{2}
to the right of
d_{1}
.
Alternatively, in terms of set partition notation

d_{1}\otimes d_{2}=d_{1}\cup\{\{b+k_{1}:b\in B\}:B\in d_{2}\}~.

Although we will use the notation only rarely, this external product is extended linearly
to a product of elements from
\mathsf{P}_{k}(x)
and
\mathsf{P}_{\ell}(x)

with the result being an element in
\mathsf{P}_{k+\ell}(x)
.

All partition diagrams can be expressed by combining the following building blocks:

It is known that
\mathsf{P}_{k}(x)
is generated by the elements
\mathsf{s}_{i}
,
\mathsf{b}_{i}
(
1\leq i<k
) and
\mathsf{p}_{j}
(
1\leq j\leq k
) depicted below (see
[
M2
, Proposition 1]
). The symbol used for
the elements does not contain an implicit value of
k
(the maximum value in the diagram)
which may need to be determined from which algebra the element lies in.

It can be easily checked that
\mathsf{e}_{i}=\mathsf{b}_{i}\mathsf{p}_{i}\mathsf{p}_{i+1}\mathsf{b}_{i}
in
\mathsf{P}_{k}(x)
.

We remark that the generators
\mathsf{s}_{i}
in
\mathsf{P}_{k}(x)
generate the symmetric group
S_{k}
. In addition,
\mathsf{p}_{i}^{2}=x\mathsf{p}_{i}
and
\mathsf{b}_{i}^{2}=\mathsf{b}_{i}
. Which implies that
\mathsf{b}_{i}
s are idempotent and the
\mathsf{p}_{i}
s are multiples of idempotents. For a complete presentation of the partition algebra see
[
HR
]
Theorem 1.11.

Assumption:
The algebra
\mathsf{P}_{k}(x)
is defined for any
x\in\mathbb{C}
.
However, we will be dividing by
x
; therefore,
we assume throughout the paper that
x\neq 0
.

2.2.
The half-partition algebra

For
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, we define
\mathsf{P}_{k+\frac{1}{2}}(x)

to be the subalgebra of
\mathsf{P}_{k+1}(x)
spanned by diagrams
d\in\widehat{\mathsf{P}}_{k+1}

such that
k+1
and
\overline{k+1}
are in the same block.
We also set
\mathsf{P}_{0}(x)=\mathbb{C}(x)
and
\mathsf{P}_{\frac{1}{2}}(x)
as the span of
\mathbf{1}
.
The set of the set partitions, where
k+1
and
\overline{k+1}

are in the same block, is closed under the diagram product.
These subalgebras were originally studied by Martin
[
M3
]

(the notation for these algebras in that paper is
P_{n}^{1}(Q)
)
but here we follow the notation of in
[
HR
]
and denote these subalgebras

\mathsf{P}_{k+\frac{1}{2}}(x)
.

The algebra
\mathsf{P}_{k+\frac{1}{2}}(x)
is generated by

\mathsf{p}_{i}
,
\mathsf{b}_{i}
and
\mathsf{s}_{j}
, where
1\leq i\leq k
and
1\leq j<k

and the elements are set partitions with maximum value
k+1,\overline{k+1}
.
Notice that
\mathsf{P}_{k+\frac{1}{2}}(x)
is a subalgebra of

\mathsf{P}_{k+1}(x)
and has all of the same generators except for
\mathsf{p}_{k+1}

and
\mathsf{s}_{k}
.
The algebra
\mathsf{P}_{k+\frac{1}{2}}(x)
has dimension
B(2k+1)
,
the number of set partitions of a set with
2k+1
elements.
There is an injection of
\mathsf{P}_{k}(x)
into
\mathsf{P}_{k+\frac{1}{2}}(x)

which sends each element
d\in\mathsf{P}_{k}(x)
to
d\otimes\mathbf{1}
.
This gives the tower of algebras:

\mathsf{P}_{0}(x)\hookrightarrow\mathsf{P}_{\frac{1}{2}}(x)\subset\mathsf{P}_{1}(x)\hookrightarrow\mathsf{P}_{1+\frac{1}{2}}(x)\subset\mathsf{P}_{2}(x)\hookrightarrow\mathsf{P}_{2+\frac{1}{2}}(x)\subset\mathsf{P}_{3}(x)\hookrightarrow\cdots~.

Example 2.2
.

Let
r\in\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, the sequence of dimensions of the algebras

\mathsf{P}_{r}(x)
for
r=0,\frac{1}{2},1,\frac{3}{2},2,\ldots
is given by
the number of set partitions of a set of size
2r
and is equal to the sequence

1,1,2,5,15,52,203,877,4140,21147,\ldots~.

2.3.
Partition algebras as centralizers

Let
V_{n}=\mathbb{C}^{n}
, the symmetric group acts on
V_{n}
via the permutation matrices

\sigma\cdot v_{i}=v_{\sigma(i)},\quad\text{ for }\sigma\in S_{n}.

Thus,
S_{n}
acts diagonally on a basis of simple tensors of
V_{n}^{\otimes k}
,

\sigma\cdot(v_{i_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes v_{i_{k}})=v_{\sigma(i_{1})}\otimes\cdots\otimes v_{\sigma(i_{k})}

The action of
\mathsf{P}_{r}(x)
on an element of
V_{n}^{\otimes k}
is given in Equation (
6.3
).
Using this action one can show the following isomorphisms.

For
n\geq 2k
,
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
,

\mathsf{P}_{k}(n)\cong\text{End}_{S_{n}}(V_{n}^{\otimes k})

and for
n\geq 2k+1
and
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
,

\mathsf{P}_{k+\frac{1}{2}}(n)\cong\text{End}_{S_{n-1}}(\text{Res}^{S_{n}}_{S_{n-1}}V_{n}^{\otimes k})~.

For details and proofs see
[
CST
,
HR
,
J3
]
.

3.
The representations of
\mathsf{P}_{k}(x)

The representation theory of the partition algebra was first studied by Martin and his collaborators,
the main references are
[
M2
,
M3
,
MS
]
and references therein.
The algebra
\mathsf{P}_{k}(x)
is semisimple
[
MS
]
whenever
x\notin\{0,1,2,\ldots,2k-2\}
. We will assume that
\mathsf{P}_{k}(x)
is semisimple and in this case we will construct “standard modules”
which form a complete set of simple modules.
In addition, we give the correspondence to set value tableaux
[
HJ
]
, when doing this we further assume that
x=n
is an integer such that
n\geq 2k
.

Recall that a block
B
is called propagating if it contains at least one element from each
[k]
and
[\overline{k}]
. Notice that the product does not increase the number of propagating blocks,
in fact, if
p(d)
denotes the number of propagating blocks and
d_{1}
and
d_{2}
are two diagrams, then

p(d_{1}d_{2})\leq\min\{p(d_{1}),p(d_{2})\}.

This gives a filtration of the algebra
\mathsf{P}_{k}(x)
by number of propagating blocks.
For
m\in[k]
, set
I_{k,m}=\{m+1,m+2,\ldots k\}
, we define the following idempotents

\tilde{\mathsf{p}}_{I_{k,m}}:=\frac{1}{x^{i}}\mathsf{p}_{m+1}\mathsf{p}_{m+2}\cdots\mathsf{p}_{k}=\raisebox{-7.22743pt}{

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}~.

This is the diagram where the vertices
m+1,m+2,\ldots,k
and

\overline{m+1},\overline{m+2},\ldots\overline{k}
are isolated.
The propagation number of
\tilde{\mathsf{p}}_{I_{k,m}}
is
m
.

Let
\mathbf{J}_{m}=\mathsf{P}_{k}(x)\tilde{\mathsf{p}}_{I_{k,m}}\mathsf{P}_{k}(x)
is the two-sided
ideal spanned by all the diagrams with at most
m
propagation blocks. This gives the filtration

\mathbf{J}_{k}\subset\mathbf{J}_{k-1}\subset\cdots\subset\mathbf{J}_{1}\subset\mathsf{P}_{k}(x).

And it can be shown that

(3.1)

\tilde{\mathsf{p}}_{I_{k,m}}\mathsf{P}_{k}(x)\tilde{\mathsf{p}}_{I_{k,m}}\cong\mathsf{P}_{m}(x)

and that

(3.2)

\mathsf{P}_{k}(x)/\mathbf{J}_{1}\cong\mathbb{C}S_{k}.

We note that the diagram elements of
\mathsf{P}_{m}(x)
that have propagating number equal to
m

are the permutations of
[m]
and the other diagrams of
\mathsf{P}_{m}(x)
have propagating
number strictly smaller than
m
.

This implies that an
S_{m}
module can be
inflated
to a
\mathsf{P}_{k}(x)
-module and by induction
using (
3.1
) and (
3.2
) and the following construction.
Given
\nu\in\mathsf{Par}_{\leq k}
and
\nu\vdash m
, let

\mathbb{S}^{\nu}
be the irreducible representation of the symmetric
group
S_{m}
. We will identify this representation with a representation
of
\mathsf{P}_{m}(x)
such that diagrams with propagation number less than
m
act by zero.
We define a
\mathsf{P}_{k}(x)
-module,

\Delta_{k}(\nu)
, by

(3.3)

\Delta_{k}(\nu)=\mathsf{P}_{k}(x)\tilde{\mathsf{p}}_{I_{k,m}}\otimes_{\mathsf{P}_{m}(x)}\mathbb{S}^{\nu},

where we have identified
\mathsf{P}_{m}(x)
with

\tilde{\mathsf{p}}_{I_{k,m}}\mathsf{P}_{k}(x)\tilde{\mathsf{p}}_{I_{k,m}}
using the isomorphism in
(
3.1
).

It then follows that the simple
\mathsf{P}_{k}(x)
modules are
indexed by the set
\mathsf{Par}_{\leq k}
containing all partitions of all non-negative integers
less than or equal to
k
.

We now describe a basis for the modules
\Delta_{k}(\nu)
for any
m\leq k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
and
\nu\vdash m
. Define
V(k,m)
to be the vector space spanned by the diagrams corresponding to set partitions of
[k]\cup[\overline{k}]
with
\overline{m+1},\ldots,\overline{k}
in singleton blocks, and all other
\overline{j}
are in propagating blocks where
\overline{j}
is the only barred element in its block. We call these
(k,m)
-diagrams
.

Example 3.1
.

An example of two diagrams in
V(6,3)
:

,
.

In what follows, we identify
\sigma\in S_{m}
with an element of the subgroup of
S_{k}

contained in
\mathsf{P}_{k}(x)
which
contains blocks
\{1,\overline{\sigma(1)}\},\ldots,\{m,\overline{\sigma(m)}\},\{m+1,\overline{m+1}\},\ldots\{k,\overline{k}\}
.

Lemma 3.2
.

If
d\in V(k,m)
, there exists
\sigma\in S_{m}
such that
d=d^{\prime}\sigma
where
d^{\prime}
satisfies the condition that
if
B_{\overline{j}}
denotes the propagating block containing
\overline{j}
, then
\text{max}(B_{\overline{j-1}}\cap[k])<\text{max}(B_{\overline{j}}\cap[k])
for all
1\leq j\leq m
.

Proof.

Let
A_{1}
,
A_{2},\ldots,A_{m}
be the propagating blocks of
d\in V(k,m)

ordered by largest element in
A_{i}\cap[k]
.
Define a permutation
\sigma\in S_{m}
, where for any
i\in[m]
,
\sigma(i)=j
if

\overline{j}\in A_{i}
.
∎

Because of Lemma
3.2
,
V(k,m)
has a natural structure of a
(\mathsf{P}_{k}(x),S_{m})
-bimodule, where
\mathsf{P}_{k}(x)
acts on the left by diagram multiplication, with the understanding that the product is zero if the resulting diagram in not in
V(k,m)
and
S_{m}
acts on the right with diagram multiplication that permutes
\overline{1},\ldots,\overline{m}
.

As vector spaces, we have

(3.4)

\Delta_{k}(\nu)\cong V(k,m)\otimes_{S_{m}}\mathbb{S}(\nu)~.

A
(k,m)
-diagram is called
(k,m)
-standard
if its propagating blocks
B_{\overline{1}},\ldots,B_{\overline{m}}
satisfy
\text{max}(B_{\overline{j-1}}\cap[k])<\text{max}(B_{\overline{j}}\cap[k])
for all
1\leq j\leq m
.

.

If
i=1
and
k=3
, then the
(3,2)
-standard diagrams are:

Recall that the irreducible representations of
S_{m}
have a basis in bijection with standard tableaux of size
m
.
For
\nu\vdash m
, the Young diagram of
\nu
is the left-justified array of boxes,
with
\nu_{i}
boxes in the
i
-th row, we use the French notation which counts rows from bottom to top.
A
standard tableau
of
\nu
is a filling of the boxes of the Young diagram of
\nu
with numbers

1,\ldots,m
so that the numbers increase along rows from left to right and along columns from bottom
to top. The
shape
of a tableau is the partition obtained by the sizes of the rows.

For
0\leq m\leq k
and
\nu\vdash m
, a basis of
\Delta_{k}(\nu)
is defined by

(3.5)

\mathcal{B}_{k}(\nu)=\{d\otimes T\,|\,d\text{ is a }(k,m)\text{-standard and }T\text{ is a standard tableau of shape }\nu\}.

A diagram
d\in\mathsf{P}_{k}(x)
acts on a basis element
d^{\prime}\otimes T
of
\Delta_{k}(\nu)
by left multiplication,

(3.6)

d\cdot d^{\prime}\otimes T=\begin{cases}dd^{\prime}\otimes T&\text{ if }p(dd^{\prime})=m\\
0&\text{otherwise}\end{cases},

in the case that
p(dd^{\prime})=m
, we use the product of diagrams and Lemma
3.2

to get
dd^{\prime}=x^{a}d_{1}\tau
where
d_{1}
is a
(k,m)
-standard diagram and
\tau\in S_{m}
.
Hence,
dd^{\prime}\otimes T=x^{a}d_{1}\otimes\tau\cdot T
, where
\tau\cdot T
might not be
standard, but can be written as a linear combination of standard tableaux using the
Garnir straightening algorithm for Specht modules (see for instance
[
S
]
).

Remark 3.4
.

Notice that
d
acts by zero if after multiplying
dd^{\prime}=x^{a}d_{1}
, we have
(1) two propagating blocks in
d^{\prime}
become connected in
d_{1}
; or
(2)
\overline{j}
for
1\leq j\leq m
does not propagate in
d_{1}
.

These modules coincide with the modules constructed by Martin
[
M2
]
in Definition 7. When
\mathsf{P}_{k}(x)
is semisimple, the set
\{\Delta_{k}(\nu)\,|\,\nu\in\mathsf{Par}_{\leq k}\}
is a complete set of non-isomorphic simple modules. We refer to these modules as the
standard
modules of the partition algebra.

By counting the elements in
\mathcal{B}_{k}(\nu)
we find the dimensions of the standard modules.

Corollary 3.5
.

(
[
BHH
]
Proposition 5.10)
For
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
and
0\leq m\leq k
, we have that

\text{dim}(\Delta_{k}(\nu))=\sum_{i=m}^{k}\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{k}{i}\binom{i}{m}f^{\nu}

where
\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{k}{i}
are the Stirling numbers of the second kind,
which enumerate the number of set partitions of
[k]
into
i
parts and
f^{\nu}
is the dimension
of
\mathbb{S}^{\nu}
, or the number of standard tableaux of shape
\nu
.

3.1.
A tableau model for the standard modules

In this
section we show that the basis
\mathcal{B}_{k}(\nu)
is in bijection with set-filled tableaux. We will set
x=n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
and
n\geq 2k
.

Definition 3.6
.

For
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
,
n\geq 2k
and
0\leq i\leq k
, let
\lambda

be a partition of
n
, a
[k]
-set valued tableau
T
of shape
\lambda

satisfies the following conditions:

(1)

The sets filling the boxes of the Young diagram of
\lambda
form a set partition
\alpha
of
[k]
, the sets in
\alpha
are called blocks.

(2)

Every box in rows
\lambda_{2},\ldots,\lambda_{\ell}
is filled with a block in
\alpha
.

(3)

Boxes at the end of the first row of
\lambda
could contain blocks of
\alpha
and, because of the
condition that
n\geq 2k
, there are at least
k
empty boxes preceding the boxes containing sets.

Let
\mathcal{T}_{k}(\lambda)
denote the set of all
[k]
-set valued tableaux of shape
\lambda
.

Example 3.7
.

The standard
[3]
-set valued tableaux in
\mathcal{T}_{3}((n-2,2))
are:

\vbox{\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0pt\halign{&\vsquare{#}\cr\vbox{\hbox{\vrule width=0.4pt\vbox to15.0pt{\hrule height=0.4pt\vss\hbox to15.0pt{\hss$1$\hss}\vss\hrule height=0.4pt}
\vrule width=0.4pt}\kern-0.4pt}\kern-0.4pt&\vbox{\hbox{\vrule width=0.4pt\vbox to15.0pt{\hrule height=0.4pt\vss\hbox to15.0pt{\hss$2$\hss}\vss\hrule height=0.4pt}
\vrule width=0.4pt}\kern-0.4pt}\kern-0.4pt\cr\vbox{\hbox{\vrule width=0.4pt\vbox to15.0pt{\hrule height=0.4pt\vss\hbox to15.0pt{\hss$$\hss}\vss\hrule height=0.4pt}
\vrule width=0.4pt}\kern-0.4pt}\kern-0.4pt&\vbox{\hbox{\vrule width=0.4pt\vbox to15.0pt{\hrule height=0.4pt\vss\hbox to15.0pt{\hss$$\hss}\vss\hrule height=0.4pt}
\vrule width=0.4pt}\kern-0.4pt}\kern-0.4pt&\vbox{\hbox{\vrule width=0.4pt\vbox to15.0pt{\hrule height=0.4pt\vss\hbox to15.0pt{\hss$$\hss}\vss\hrule height=0.4pt}

where each of the tableaux listed above have a first row with
n-2
boxes.

Definition 3.8
.

Let
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
,
n\geq 2k
, and
0\leq m\leq k
, and
\nu\vdash m
. We know define a map

\rho:\mathcal{B}_{k}(\nu)\rightarrow\mathcal{T}_{k}((n-|\nu|,\nu))
for
d\otimes T\in\mathcal{B}_{k}(\nu)

assign a
[k]
-set valued tableau as follows:

(1)

For each propagating block in
d
,
replace
j
in
T
by the block
B_{\overline{j}}
containing
\overline{j}
.

(2)

Create a set valued tableau of shape
(n-|\nu|,\nu)

by adding a first row of length
n-|\nu|
to the tableaux obtained in step (1).

(3)

Place all non-propagating blocks in the top of
d
in last-letter order in the boxes at the end of the row
n-|\nu|
added in step (2).

It is relatively easy to see that the map
\rho
is invertible.
The sets in the first row of the tableau correspond to non-propagating blocks
in the
(k,m)
-standard diagram.
The propagating blocks in the
(k,m)
-standard diagram
are determined by connecting
\overline{j}
to the
j^{th}
largest set (by maximum value in the set)
for
1\leq j\leq m
.
The standard tableau is determined once the
(k,m)
-standard diagram is known. Therefore,
\rho
is a bijection.

Example 3.9
.

Correspondence between a basis element
d\otimes T\in\Delta_{9}((2,1))
and a
[9]
-set valued
tableau of shape
(n-3,2,1)
.

Using the notation from
[
HJ
]
, we let for any
\nu\vdash m
, with
0\leq m\leq k

\mathsf{P}_{k}^{\nu}=\mathbb{C}\text{-Span}\{N_{S}:S\in\mathcal{T}_{k}((n-|\nu|,\nu))\}~.

In
[
HJ
]
Definition 4.7, the authors define an action of the diagrams in
\mathsf{P}_{k}(n)
on
\mathsf{P}_{k}^{\nu}
. In Section 3 of
[
HJ
]
, the authors show that
\mathsf{P}_{k}^{\nu}\cong\Delta_{k}(\nu)
as
\mathsf{P}_{k}(n)
-modules, in particular they called the
(k,m)
-standard diagrams “noncrossing
m
-factors”. We skip these details here as we are interested in tableaux to serve as indexing sets for bases to help describe the dimensions of the modules. For example, we can see that Corollary
3.5
is counting the number of
[k]
-set valued tableaux of shape
(n-|\nu|,\nu)
.

Remark 3.10
.

Let
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
,
n\geq 2k
, and
0\leq m\leq k
, and
\nu\vdash m
.
The map
\rho:\mathcal{B}_{k}(\nu)\rightarrow\mathcal{T}_{k}((n-|\nu|,\nu))
is a bijection. In Section
5
, we will use the linear extension of
\rho
and apply it to linear combinations.

3.2.
Representations of the half-partition algebra

The irreducible representations of the half-algebras using tableaux was not considered in
[
HJ
]

but Halverson-Ram
[
HR
]
constructed the matrix units for the half-partition algebras using Jones’ basic construction.

In this section we describe the standard modules and then give a correspondence to tableaux modules. The construction of the simple standard modules of
\mathsf{P}_{k+\frac{1}{2}}(x)
is similar to that of
\mathsf{P}_{k}(x)
. In this section we assume
x\geq 2k+2
. The main difference in the construction is that we use the idempotent

\tilde{\mathsf{p}}_{I_{k,m}}\otimes\mathbf{1}
. We define left modules for any
\nu\vdash m
as follows

\Delta_{k+\frac{1}{2}}(\nu)=\mathsf{P}_{k+\frac{1}{2}}(x)(\tilde{\mathsf{p}}_{I_{k,m}}\otimes\mathbf{1})\otimes_{\mathsf{P}_{{m}+\frac{1}{2}}(x)}\mathbb{S}^{\nu}

where we have used the corresponding identifications as we did in the construction of the standard modules for
\mathsf{P}_{k}(x)
.

To define a linear basis for this module, we first define a vector space

V_{\frac{1}{2}}(k+1,m)
as the span of all diagrams with exactly
m+1
propagating blocks,
each containing exactly one bottom element in the set

\{\overline{1},\ldots,\overline{m},\overline{k+1}\}
,
and the vertices
\{\overline{m+1},\ldots,\overline{k}\}
are singletons.

Example 3.11
.

Elements in
V_{\frac{1}{2}}(8,3)
:

,
.

The
half-
(k+1,m)
-standard diagrams
are defined to be the diagrams where if
B_{j}
and
B_{j+1}
are propagating blocks containing
\overline{j}
and
\overline{j+1}
, respectively, then
\text{max}(B_{j}\cap[k])<\text{max}(B_{j+1}\cap[k])
.

Example 3.12
.

(3,1)
-standard diagrams are:

Similarly as in Lemma
3.2
, we have that every
d\in V_{\frac{1}{2}}(k+1,m)
can be written as

d=d^{\prime}\sigma,

where
\sigma\in S_{m}
and
d^{\prime}
is a half-
(k+1,m)
-standard diagram.
With these definitions, let
\nu\vdash m
, then as vector spaces, we have

\Delta_{k+\frac{1}{2}}(\nu)\cong V_{\frac{1}{2}}(k+1,m)\otimes_{S_{m}}\mathbb{S}^{\nu}.

A linear basis for this module is

\mathcal{B}_{k+\frac{1}{2}}(\nu)
consisting of elements
d\otimes T
where
d

is a half-
(k+1,m)
-standard diagram and
T
is a standard tableau of shape
\nu
.
The action of
d\in\mathsf{P}_{k+\frac{1}{2}}(x)
is

(3.7)

d\cdot d^{\prime}\otimes T=\begin{cases}dd^{\prime}\otimes T&\text{ if }p(dd^{\prime})=m+1\\
0&\text{otherwise}\end{cases},

Here, if
p(dd^{\prime})=m+1
, then
dd^{\prime}\in V_{\frac{1}{2}}(k+1,m)
and it can be written as
dd^{\prime}=d_{1}\tau
, where
d_{1}
is half-
(k+1,m)
-standard diagram and
\tau\in S_{m}
. In this case,
dd^{\prime}\otimes T=d_{1}\otimes\tau\cdot T
, and as before we apply Garnir straightening algorithm to write
\tau\cdot T
as a linear combination of standard tableaux, if needed.

\raisebox{-4.33601pt}{
\vbox{\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0pt\halign{&\vsquare{#}\cr\vbox{\hbox{\vrule width=0.4pt\vbox to14.0pt{\hrule height=0.4pt\vss\hbox to14.0pt{\hss$2$\hss}\vss\hrule height=0.4pt}

\vrule width=0.4pt}\kern-0.4pt}\kern-0.4pt\cr}}}~}\right\}~.

Corresponding, respectively to

\Delta_{2+\frac{1}{2}}(\emptyset)
,
\Delta_{2+\frac{1}{2}}((1))
,

\Delta_{2+\frac{1}{2}}((2))
, and
\Delta_{2+\frac{1}{2}}((1,1))
.

The elements in
\mathcal{B}_{k+\frac{1}{2}}(\nu)
are in bijection with pairs of set valued tableaux of shape
((n-1-|\nu|,\nu),(1))
, where
d\otimes T\in\mathcal{B}_{\frac{1}{2}}(\nu)
corresponds to the pair constructed as follows:

(1)

The propagating blocks of
d
containing
\overline{1},\ldots,\overline{m}
are inserted in the boxes of
T
where
j
is replaced by
B_{\overline{j}}\cap[k]
.

(2)

The nonpropagating blocks in
d
are inserted in last letter order at the end of the first row.

(3)

B_{\overline{k+1}}\cap[k]
is inserted in the one box in the second component.

Example 3.14
.

Correspondence between a basis element
d\otimes T\in\Delta_{8+\frac{1}{2}}((2,1))
and a pair of set valued
tableaux of shape
((n-3,1,1),(1))
.

Remark 3.15
.

The Bratteli diagram of the tower

\mathsf{P}_{0}(x)\hookrightarrow\mathsf{P}_{1}(x)\hookrightarrow\mathsf{P}_{2}(x)\hookrightarrow\cdots

has in general multiple edges between the vertices since tensoring representations of the
symmetric group by the permutation module is not multiplicity free.
The introduction of the half algebras helps decompose the tensor product of the permutation
module into two operations, induction and restriction.
The Bratteli diagram for the tower

\mathsf{P}_{0}(x)\hookrightarrow\mathsf{P}_{\frac{1}{2}}(x)\subset\mathsf{P}_{1}(x)\hookrightarrow\mathsf{P}_{1+\frac{1}{2}}(x)\subset\cdots

does not have multiple edges between the vertices.
The Bratteli diagram for partition algebras interlaced
with half-partition algebras is in
[
HR
]
, page 893.

4.
Quasi-partition algebras and half quasi-partition algebras

For
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{k>0}
, the quasi-partition algebra
\mathsf{QP}_{k}(n)
was introduced in
[
DO
]

as the centralizer algebra

\text{End}_{S_{n}}((\mathbb{S}^{(n-1,1)})^{\otimes k})
,
where
\mathbb{S}^{(n-1,1)}
is the irreducible representation of the symmetric group,
S_{n}
.
In this section, we give a more general definition and introduce
the half quasi-partition algebras,
\mathsf{QP}_{k+\frac{1}{2}}(x)
.

We define
\pi:=\mathbf{1}-\frac{1}{x}\mathsf{p}
and using tensor notation we define an idempotent
\pi^{\otimes k}
in
\mathsf{P}_{k}(x)
as follows

(4.1)

\pi^{\otimes k}:=\left(\mathbf{1}^{\otimes k}-\frac{1}{x}\mathsf{p}_{1}\right)\left(\mathbf{1}^{\otimes k}-\frac{1}{x}\mathsf{p}_{2}\right)\cdots\left(\mathbf{1}^{\otimes k}-\frac{1}{x}\mathsf{p}_{k}\right).

Let
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
and let
J
be any subset of

[k]=\{1,2,\ldots,k\}
,
we set
\mathsf{p}_{\emptyset}:=\mathbf{1}^{\otimes k}

and
\mathsf{p}_{J}:=\prod_{j\in J}\mathsf{p}_{j}
. Using this notation we have

(4.2)

\pi^{\otimes k}=\sum_{J\subseteq[k]}\frac{1}{(-x)^{|J|}}\mathsf{p}_{J}~.

The corresponding idempotent in

\mathsf{P}_{k+\frac{1}{2}}(x)\subseteq\mathsf{P}_{k+1}(x)
will
be denoted by
\pi^{\otimes k}_{k+1}:=\pi^{\otimes k}\otimes\bf{1}
to indicate that it is contained in the larger algebra.

Example 4.1
.

The idempotent in
\mathsf{P}_{3}(x)
is

\pi^{\otimes 3}=\mathbf{1}^{\otimes 3}-\frac{1}{x}\mathsf{p}_{1}-\frac{1}{x}\mathsf{p}_{2}-\frac{1}{x}\mathsf{p}_{3}+\frac{1}{x^{2}}\mathsf{p}_{1}\mathsf{p}_{2}+\frac{1}{x^{2}}\mathsf{p}_{1}\mathsf{p}_{3}+\frac{1}{x^{2}}\mathsf{p}_{2}\mathsf{p}_{3}-\frac{1}{x^{3}}\mathsf{p}_{1}\mathsf{p}_{2}\mathsf{p}_{3}
.
This element expressed using diagrams is

\displaystyle\pi^{\otimes 3}=

The idempotent in
\mathsf{P}_{2+\frac{1}{2}}(x)
is

\pi^{\otimes 2}_{3}=\mathbf{1}^{\otimes 3}-\frac{1}{x}\mathsf{p}_{1}-\frac{1}{x}\mathsf{p}_{2}+\frac{1}{x^{2}}\mathsf{p}_{1}\mathsf{p}_{2}
and this expression in terms of diagrams is

For
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}
and any diagram
d\in\widehat{\mathsf{P}}_{k}
, we define

\overline{d}=\pi^{\otimes k}d\pi^{\otimes k}.

And similarly, for
d\in\widehat{\mathsf{P}}_{k+\frac{1}{2}}
, we define

\overline{d}=\pi^{\otimes k}_{k+1}d\pi^{\otimes k}_{k+1}
.

Lemma 4.2
.

For
r\in\frac{1}{2}{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}
if

d\in\widehat{\mathsf{P}}_{r}
is a diagram with one or more singletons, then
\overline{d}=0
.

Proof.

Observe that
\mathsf{p}_{i}\pi^{\otimes k}=\pi^{\otimes k}\mathsf{p}_{i}=(\mathbf{1}^{\otimes k}-\frac{1}{x}\mathsf{p}_{1})\cdots(\mathsf{p}_{i}-\mathsf{p}_{i})\cdots(\mathbf{1}^{\otimes k}-\frac{1}{x}\mathsf{p}_{k})=0
,
since the
\mathsf{p}_{i}
s commute and
\mathsf{p}_{i}^{2}=x\mathsf{p}_{i}
.
If
d
has a singleton
\{i\}
on the top row,
then
d=\frac{1}{x}\mathsf{p}_{i}d
and if
d
has a singleton
\{\overline{i}\}
in the bottom row,
then
d=\frac{1}{x}d\mathsf{p}_{i}
.
Hence
\overline{d}=0
if
d
has any singletons, since
\mathsf{P}_{r}(x)
is associative.

The same proof also applies to
\pi^{\otimes k}_{k+1}
since it is equal to
\pi^{\otimes k}\otimes\mathbf{1}

and
k+1
,
\overline{k+1}
are not isolated in
\mathsf{P}_{k+\frac{1}{2}}(x)
.
∎

In the following proposition, we give an expression for the elements
\overline{d}
, where
d
is a diagram without singletons. For this we need some notation and definitions.
For
r\in\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
and
d,d^{\prime}\in\widehat{\mathsf{P}}_{r}
, we say that
d^{\prime}
is

finer
than
d
(alternatively,
d
is
coarser
than
d^{\prime}
)
and use the notation
d^{\prime}\leq d
if every block of
d^{\prime}
is contained in a block of
d
.
Furthermore for a block
B\in d
,
we set
sp_{d^{\prime}}(B)=\{B\cap B^{\prime}:B^{\prime}\in d^{\prime}\}
, the intersections of a single block of
d
with all the blocks of
d^{\prime}
. We write
d^{\prime}\leq_{\ast}d
if
d^{\prime}\leq d
and

sp_{d^{\prime}}(B)
contains at most one set of size greater or equal to two for each
B\in d
.
For every block
B
of
d
, we define the
length
of
B
in
d^{\prime}
, denoted by
\ell_{d^{\prime}}(B)
,
to be the number of blocks of size
1
in
sp_{d^{\prime}}(B)
. We note that if
d^{\prime}\leq d
, then

\ell_{d^{\prime}}(B)=|B|
if and only if all elements
i\in B
are isolated vertices
in
d^{\prime}
.

We note that

d^{\prime}\leq_{\ast}d
and
d^{\prime\prime}\leq_{\ast}d
. Let
B_{1}=\{1,2,\overline{1}\}
and
B_{2}=\{3,\overline{2},\overline{3}\}
so that

d=\{B_{1},B_{2}\}
, then

\ell_{d^{\prime}}(B_{1})=|B_{1}|=3
,
\ell_{d^{\prime}}(B_{2})=1
,
\ell_{d^{\prime\prime}}(B_{1})=1
and
\ell_{d^{\prime\prime}}(B_{2})=0
.

Proposition 4.4
.

Let
r\in\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
and
let
d\in\widehat{\mathsf{P}}_{r}
be a diagram without singletons, then

\overline{d}=d+\sum_{d^{\prime}:d^{\prime}<_{\ast}d}a_{d,d^{\prime}}(x)d^{\prime}~,

where

a_{d,d^{\prime}}(x)=\prod_{B\in d}\begin{cases}\frac{|B|-1}{(-x)^{\ell_{d^{\prime}}(B)-1}}&\hbox{ if }\ell_{d^{\prime}}(B)=|B|\\
\frac{1}{(-x)^{\ell_{d^{\prime}}(B)}}&\hbox{ otherwise}\end{cases}~.

Proof.

Let
d\in\widehat{\mathsf{P}}_{r}
be a diagram without singletons, then by Equation (
4.2
),

\overline{d}
is a sum of terms involving diagrams
in the expression

\frac{1}{(-x)^{|J|+|J^{\prime}|}}\mathsf{p}_{J}d\mathsf{p}_{J^{\prime}}

for some subsets
J,J^{\prime}\subseteq[k]
.
Let
d^{\prime}
be the diagram that occurs when we multiply
\mathsf{p}_{J}d\mathsf{p}_{J^{\prime}}
. Multiplying
d
on the top by
\mathsf{p}_{J}
isolates all vertices in
J
and multiplying on the bottom by
\mathsf{p}_{J^{\prime}}
isolated all vertices in
J^{\prime}
in the bottom of
d
. The resulting
d^{\prime}
is finer than
d
and results from isolating some vertices of blocks in
d
. Hence, for each block
B\in d
, all elements in
B
that also are either in
J
or
in
J^{\prime}
are singletons in
d^{\prime}
. The only set in
sp_{d^{\prime}}(B)
that may contain more than one element is
B\backslash(J\cup J^{\prime})
.
Therefore by definition,
d^{\prime}\leq_{\ast}d
.

Now fix a
d^{\prime}
such that
d^{\prime}<_{\ast}d
.
Assume that
d^{\prime}
is the diagram of the term in the expression
\mathsf{p}_{J}d\mathsf{p}_{J^{\prime}}
.
For each block
B
, we let
J_{B}=J\cap B
(and respectively
J^{\prime}_{B}=J^{\prime}\cap B
).
We have therefore

\mathsf{p}_{J}=\left(\prod_{B\in d}\mathsf{p}_{J_{B}}\right)
(with a similar expression for
\mathsf{p}_{J^{\prime}}
).
In determining the coefficient of
d^{\prime}
in
\overline{d}
, we will calculate the contribution
to the coefficient grouped by possible pairs of sets
(J_{B},J^{\prime}_{B})
.

For each block of
B\in d
, either
sp_{d^{\prime}}(B)
contains
a block of size greater than one or it contains only singletons.
If
sp_{d^{\prime}}(B)
contains
a block of size greater than one, then there are unique
J_{B}
and
J^{\prime}_{B}

that will appear in every term where
d^{\prime}
is the diagram in the expression
\mathsf{p}_{J}d\mathsf{p}_{J^{\prime}}
.
Those terms will have a contribution of
\frac{1}{(-x)^{|J_{B}|+|J_{B^{\prime}}|}}=\frac{1}{(-x)^{\ell_{d^{\prime}}(B)}}

from the part corresponding to this block.

If
sp_{d^{\prime}}(B)
contains only singletons, then there are
|B|+1
pairs of sets
(J_{B},J_{B}^{\prime})

such that
J_{B}\subseteq B\cap[k]
and
J^{\prime}\subseteq B\cap[\overline{k}]
where

d^{\prime}
is the diagram in the expression
\mathsf{p}_{J}d\mathsf{p}_{J^{\prime}}
.
Those pairs of sets are:
((B\cap[k])\backslash\{a\},B\cap[\overline{k}])

for
a\in B\cap[k]
,

(B\cap[k],(B\cap[\overline{k}])\backslash\{a^{\prime}\})

for
a^{\prime}\in B\cap[\overline{k}]
, and
(B\cap[k],B\cap[\overline{k}])
.
In the first two cases, the contribution to the coefficient will be

\frac{1}{(-x)^{|J_{B}|+|J_{B^{\prime}}|}}=\frac{1}{(-x)^{\ell_{d^{\prime}}(B)-1}}
.
In the latter case
\frac{1}{(-x)^{|J_{B}|+|J_{B^{\prime}}|}}=\frac{1}{(-x)^{\ell_{d^{\prime}}(B)}}
, however

\mathsf{p}_{J}d\mathsf{p}_{J^{\prime}}
will contribute a power of
x
due
to the diagram product that will have a disconnected component in the middle row
and hence the contribution from that term will be
\frac{x}{(-x)^{\ell_{d^{\prime}}(B)}}=\frac{-1}{(-x)^{\ell_{d^{\prime}}(B)-1}}
.
The sum over all of the
|B|+1
possible pairs
(J_{B},J_{B}^{\prime})
will contribute
a total of
\frac{|B|-1}{(-x)^{\ell_{d^{\prime}}(B)-1}}
to the coefficient
of
d^{\prime}
in
\overline{d}
.

Because the pairs
(J_{B},J^{\prime}_{B})
can be chosen for each block
B\in d
, we
conclude that the coefficient of
d^{\prime}
in
\overline{d}
is equal to
the expression stated in the proposition.
∎

Referring to Example
4.3
the diagram corresponding to
d^{\prime}
has coefficients
\frac{2}{(-x)^{2}}\frac{1}{(-x)^{1}}
and the coefficient of the diagram
d^{\prime\prime}
is
\frac{1}{(-x)^{1}}\frac{1}{(-x)^{0}}
.

4.1.
The product in quasi-partition algebras

For
r\in\frac{1}{2}{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}
, we set

\widehat{\mathsf{D}}_{r}=\{d:d\in\widehat{\mathsf{P}}_{r}\hbox{ without singletons}\},

and define

(4.3)

\mathsf{QP}_{r}(x)=\mathbb{C}(x)\text{-Span}\{\overline{d}\,|\,d\in\widehat{\mathsf{D}}_{r}\}.

If
r
is an integer, we call
\mathsf{QP}_{r}(x)
the
quasi-partition algebra
and if
r
is half an integer, the
half quasi-partition algebra
.

We now show that
\mathsf{QP}_{r}(x)

for
r\in{\frac{1}{2}}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
is closed under the product.

Proposition 4.6
.

Let
d_{1},d_{2}\in\widehat{\mathsf{D}}_{k}
and suppose
d_{1}d_{2}=x^{a}d_{3}
in
\mathsf{P}_{k}(x)
, where
a\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
. Then, we have either
\overline{d_{1}}\ \overline{d_{2}}=0
or

\overline{d_{1}}\ \overline{d_{2}}=\sum_{d:d\leq d_{3}}c_{d_{1},d_{2}}^{d}(x)\overline{d},\qquad c_{d_{1},d_{2}}^{d}(x)\in\mathbb{C}(x),

where
d\leq d^{\prime}
means that
d
is finer than or equal to
d^{\prime}
, and
d\in\widehat{\mathsf{D}}_{r}
.

Proof.

Suppose that
r=k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
.
The product
\overline{d_{1}}\ \overline{d_{2}}=\pi^{\otimes k}(d_{1}\pi^{\otimes k}d_{2})\pi^{\otimes k}=\overline{d_{1}\pi^{\otimes k}d_{2}}
, the first equality follows because
\pi^{\otimes k}
is an idempotent and the last expression simply means that we multiply every element in the linear combination
d_{1}\pi^{\otimes k}d_{2}
on the right and on the left by
\pi^{\otimes k}
.

We have
d_{1}\pi^{\otimes k}d_{2}=\sum_{J\subseteq[k]}\frac{1}{(-x)^{|J|}}d_{1}\mathsf{p}_{J}d_{2}
. Each summand
d_{1}\mathsf{p}_{J}d_{2}
can be computed by drawing a three layered graph with
d_{1}
on top, followed by
\mathsf{p}_{J}
, and
d_{2}
in the bottom. For any
J\neq\emptyset
, we get that the three layered graph always has fewer edges than the graph of
d_{1}\mathsf{p}_{\emptyset}d_{2}=d_{1}d_{2}
(since
\mathsf{p}_{\emptyset}=\mathbf{1}^{\otimes k}
, the identity in
\mathsf{P}_{k}(x)
). Hence after removing middle blocks we get
d_{1}\mathsf{p}_{J}d_{2}=x^{m}d^{\prime}
, where
d^{\prime}
is finer or equal to the diagram that occurs in the product
d_{1}d_{2}
, i.e.
d_{3}
. After combining like terms, we have

d_{1}\pi^{\otimes k}d_{2}=\sum_{d^{\prime}\leq d_{3}}\gamma_{d_{1},d_{2}}^{d^{\prime}}(x)d^{\prime}.

Now multiplying this last expression on the left and on the right by
\pi^{\otimes k}
we get

\overline{d_{1}}\ \overline{d_{2}}=\overline{d_{1}\pi^{\otimes k}d_{2}}=\sum_{d^{\prime}\leq d_{3}}\gamma_{d_{1},d_{2}}^{d^{\prime}}(x)\overline{d^{\prime}}.

It is possible for the last expression to be equal to zero in the case that all terms cancel out or when all resulting diagrams have singletons. If a term
\gamma_{d_{1},d_{2}}^{d^{\prime}}(x)\overline{d^{\prime}}
is nonzero, the diagram
d^{\prime}
has no singletons by Lemma
4.2
.

For
r=k+\frac{1}{2}
the above argument also works when we use the idempotent
\pi_{k+1}^{\otimes k}
to show that we get finer elements in the summand; however, we have to show that we get
\overline{d^{\prime}}
where
d^{\prime}
has
k+1
and
\overline{k+1}
in the same block. But this should be clear as in this case
d_{1},d_{2},\pi_{k+1}^{\otimes k},\in\mathsf{P}_{k+\frac{1}{2}}(x)
and
\mathsf{P}_{k+\frac{1}{2}}(x)
is closed under the product, therefore every resulting summand satisfies this condition.
∎

\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \color[rgb]{0,0,0}{}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 43.31 21.65 M 44.83 21.65 C 44.83 22.49 44.15 23.18 43.31 23.18 C 42.47 23.18 41.79 22.49 41.79 21.65 C 41.79 20.81 42.47 20.13 43.31 20.13 C 44.15 20.13 44.83 20.81 44.83 21.65 Z M 43.31 21.65}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}~.

We note that
\mathsf{QP}_{k}(x)
is an algebra with unity
\pi^{\otimes k}
and
\mathsf{QP}_{k+\frac{1}{2}}(x)
with unity
\pi^{\otimes k}_{k+1}
.

For any permutation
\tau
of
[k]
, and any
J\subseteq[k]
, let
\tau(J)=\{\tau(i)\,|\,i\in J\}
.
Any permutation
\sigma\in S_{k}
is identified with a diagram in
\mathsf{P}_{k}(x)
corresponding to the
set partition with blocks
\{i,\overline{\sigma(i)}\}
. For any
\sigma\in S_{k}\subseteq\mathsf{P}_{k}(x)

a permutation and
J\subseteq[k]
, we have

\mathsf{p}_{J}\sigma=\sigma\mathsf{p}_{\sigma(J)}\quad\text{ and }\quad\pi^{\otimes k}=\sum_{J\subseteq[k]}\frac{1}{(-x)^{|\sigma(J)|}}\mathsf{p}_{\sigma(J)}.

From these two identities, it follows that

(4.4)

\pi^{\otimes k}\sigma=\sigma\pi^{\otimes k}

Hence,
\overline{\sigma}=\pi^{\otimes k}\sigma\pi^{\otimes k}=\pi^{\otimes k}\sigma
as an element in
\mathsf{QP}_{k}(x)
.

Proposition 4.8
.

The elements
\overline{\mathsf{s}_{i}}
for
1\leq i\leq k-1
satisfy the following relations:

\overline{\mathsf{s}_{i}}^{2}=\pi^{\otimes k},\quad\overline{\mathsf{s}_{i}}\ \overline{\mathsf{s}_{i+1}}\ \overline{\mathsf{s}_{i}}=\overline{\mathsf{s}_{i+1}}\ \overline{\mathsf{s}_{i}}\ \overline{\mathsf{s}_{i+1}},\quad\text{and }\quad\overline{\mathsf{s}_{i}}\ \overline{\mathsf{s}_{j}}=\overline{\mathsf{s}_{j}}\ \overline{\mathsf{s}_{i}}~,

where
|i-j|>1
. Hence, the
\overline{\mathsf{s}_{i}}
generate a group isomorphic to
S_{k}
with identity
\pi^{\otimes k}
in
\mathsf{QP}_{k}(x)
.

Proof.

By Equation (
4.4
), if
\sigma,\tau\in S_{k}\subset\mathsf{P}_{k}(x)
, then
\overline{\sigma}\ \overline{\tau}=\pi^{\otimes k}\sigma\tau=\overline{\sigma\tau}
. From this and the relations of the
s_{i}
’s in
\mathsf{P}_{k}(x)
.
∎

4.2.
A tower of quasi-partition algebras

As in the case of the half-partition algebra, for
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}

there is a natural embedding of
\mathsf{QP}_{k}(x)
into
\mathsf{QP}_{k+\frac{1}{2}}(x)

by the embedding
\overline{d}\mapsto\overline{d}\otimes\mathbf{1}
.
However for
k
an integer it is not the case that
\mathsf{QP}_{k+\frac{1}{2}}(x)
is a subalgebra
of
\mathsf{QP}_{k+1}(x)
(see Example
4.9
).
Instead we describe a two-step process which consists of
an inclusion followed by a projection.

For integers
k\geq 0
, and
d\in\mathsf{P}_{k+1}(x)
, we define
1
1

1

Note the difference between
the definition of
\tilde{d}
and
\overline{d}=\pi^{\otimes k+1}d\pi^{\otimes k+1}
.

\tilde{d}=\pi^{\otimes k}_{k+1}d\pi^{\otimes k}_{k+1}~.

Now consider the subalgebra of
\mathsf{P}_{k+1}(x)
,

\widetilde{\mathsf{QP}}_{k+1}(x)=\mathbb{C}(x)\text{-Span}\{\tilde{d}\,|\,d\in\mathsf{P}_{k+1}(x)\}~.

We note that the basis of
\widetilde{\mathsf{QP}}_{k+1}(x)
is:

(4.5)

\{\widetilde{d}:d\in\widehat{\mathsf{P}}_{k+1}\hbox{ has no singletons in }[k]\cup[\overline{k}]\}~.

The index set are the diagrams which have no singletons in the first
k
positions
but that may have singletons in the last position. Equivalently it could be expressed
as neither
\{i\}
nor
\{\overline{i}\}
is a block for
1\leq i\leq k
.

Hence, the first step is the natural inclusion of
\mathsf{QP}_{k+\frac{1}{2}}(x)
in

\widetilde{\mathsf{QP}}_{k+1}(x)
. It should be made clear that

\widetilde{\mathsf{QP}}_{k+1}(x)
is larger than
both
\mathsf{QP}_{k+\frac{1}{2}}(x)
and
\mathsf{QP}_{k+1}(x)
since, for instance,

\pi^{\otimes k}_{k+1}\mathsf{p}_{k+1}\in\widetilde{\mathsf{QP}}_{k+1}(x)

but it is not an element of either
\mathsf{QP}_{k+\frac{1}{2}}(x)
or
\mathsf{QP}_{k+1}(x)
.

Thus far we have introduced algebras in our tower so that for each
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}
,

\displaystyle\mathsf{QP}_{k}(x)

\displaystyle=\pi^{\otimes k}\mathsf{P}_{k}(x)\pi^{\otimes k}~,

(4.6)

\displaystyle\mathsf{QP}_{k+\frac{1}{2}}(x)

\displaystyle=\pi^{\otimes k}_{k+1}\mathsf{P}_{k+\frac{1}{2}}(x)\pi^{\otimes k}_{k+1}~,

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathsf{QP}}_{k+1}(x)

\displaystyle=\pi^{\otimes k}_{k+1}\mathsf{P}_{k+1}(x)\pi^{\otimes k}_{k+1}~.

Then for the second step there is a projection from
\widetilde{\mathsf{QP}}_{k+1}(x)

to
\mathsf{QP}_{k+1}(x)
which, for each
d\in\widehat{\mathsf{P}}_{k+1}
,

\tilde{d}\in\widetilde{\mathsf{QP}}_{k+1}(x)

is sent to
\overline{d}=(\mathbf{1}^{\otimes k+1}-\frac{1}{x}\mathsf{p}_{k+1})\tilde{d}(\mathbf{1}^{\otimes k+1}-\frac{1}{x}\mathsf{p}_{k+1})\in\mathsf{QP}_{k+1}(x)
.

Therefore we have the following chain of inclusions and projections:

(4.7)

\mathsf{QP}_{0}(x)\hookrightarrow\mathsf{QP}_{\frac{1}{2}}(x)\subseteq\widetilde{\mathsf{QP}}_{1}(x)\twoheadrightarrow\mathsf{QP}_{1}(x)\hookrightarrow\mathsf{QP}_{1+\frac{1}{2}}(x)\subseteq\widetilde{\mathsf{QP}}_{2}(x)\twoheadrightarrow\mathsf{QP}_{2}(x)\hookrightarrow\cdots~.

While there is an embedding of
\mathsf{QP}_{k}(x)
in
\mathsf{QP}_{k+\frac{1}{2}}(x)

by tensoring by
\mathbf{1}
, the
following example makes it clearer that
\mathsf{QP}_{k+\frac{1}{2}}(x)

is not a subalgebra of
\mathsf{QP}_{k+1}(x)
.

Example 4.9
.

Notice that
\mathsf{QP}_{1+\frac{1}{2}}(x)
is an algebra of dimension
2
and is spanned by
the following two elements:

A basis for the algebra
\widetilde{\mathsf{QP}}_{2}(x)
contains seven elements.
Two of those elements are the same as those that are in
\mathsf{QP}_{1+\frac{1}{2}}(x)
,

In displaying these elements, we see that
\mathsf{QP}_{1+\frac{1}{2}}(x)
is not
naturally a subalgebra of
\mathsf{QP}_{2}(x)
as it was in the case of the
partition algebra.

The dimensions of these algebras are determined by counting the elements
in Equations (
4.3
) and (
4.5
).

The sequence of dimensions of
\dim(\mathsf{QP}_{k}(x))
for
k
an integer was shown in

[
DO
]
to be

\text{dim}(\mathsf{QP}_{k}(x))=\sum_{j=1}^{2k}(-1)^{j-1}B(2k-j)+1

and are every other term in the
[
OEIS
]
sequence
A000296
.
The dimension is equal to the number of set partitions of

[k]\cup[\overline{k}]
without blocks of size one.

The next corollary shows that the sequence of dimensions of

\dim(\mathsf{QP}_{k+\frac{1}{2}}(x))
for
k
an integer
is
B(2k)
.
This sequence is every other term in the
[
OEIS
]

sequence
A000110
.

The sequence of dimensions of
\widetilde{\mathsf{QP}}_{k+1}(x)
is
given by
[
OEIS
]
sequence
A207978
.
Using the standard counting techniques of inclusion-exclusion we deduce that

{\mathrm{dim}}(\widetilde{\mathsf{QP}}_{k+1}(x))=\sum_{s=0}^{2k}(-1)^{s}\binom{2k}{s}B(2k+2-s)~.

The expression
\binom{2k}{s}B(2k+2-s)
represents the number of set partitions of

[k+1]\cup[\overline{k+1}]
where a subset of
s
of the vertices in

[k]\cup[\overline{k}]
are isolated
and a there is a set partition on the remaining vertices.

Example 4.10
.

The sequence of dimensions of the algebras for
0\leq k\leq 6
is given in the table below.

Corollary 4.11
.

For
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
,
the set
\{\overline{d}\,|\,d\in\widehat{\mathsf{D}}_{k+\frac{1}{2}}\}

is a basis for
\mathsf{QP}_{k+\frac{1}{2}}(x)
and the
dimension of
\mathsf{QP}_{k+\frac{1}{2}}(x)
is equal to
B(2k)
,
the number of set partitions of a set of size
2k
.

Proof.

We will show that
\left|\widehat{\mathsf{D}}_{k+\frac{1}{2}}\right|=\left|\widehat{\mathsf{P}}_{k}\right|

by establishing a bijection between these two sets.

Fix
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, then
the dimension of
\mathsf{QP}_{k+\frac{1}{2}}(x)
is equal to the number of elements in

\widehat{\mathsf{D}}_{k+\frac{1}{2}}
since by
Proposition
4.4
the
\overline{d}

for
d\in\widehat{\mathsf{D}}_{k+\frac{1}{2}}
are linearly independent.

Define
F:\widehat{\mathsf{P}}_{k}\rightarrow\widehat{\mathsf{D}}_{k+\frac{1}{2}}
for any set partition
d\in\widehat{\mathsf{P}}_{k}
by
placing all singletons in
d
in the block containing
\{k+1,\overline{k+1}\}
, that is

F(d)=\{S\in d\text{ such that }|S|>1,\}\cup\{\{a\,|\,\{a\}\in d\}\cup\{k+1,\overline{k+1}\}\}.

Since
F
is invertible, it follows that
\left|\widehat{\mathsf{P}}_{k}\right|=\left|\widehat{\mathsf{D}}_{k+\frac{1}{2}}\right|=B(2k)

and it is equal to the dimension of
\mathsf{QP}_{k+\frac{1}{2}}(x)
.
∎

5.
Irreducible representations of quasi-partition algebras

In this section we will use the representations constructed for

\mathsf{P}_{k}(x)
and
\mathsf{P}_{k+\frac{1}{2}}(x)
in Section
3

and the projections
\pi^{\otimes k}
and
\pi_{k+1}^{\otimes k}
to construct
the representations of
\mathsf{QP}_{k}(x)
.

A different approach was taken by Scrimshaw
[
Sc
, Theorem 3.2]

where he shows that that the quasi-partition
algebra as a subalgebra of the partition algebra
\mathsf{P}_{k}(x-1)

is a cellular algebra and hence is semi-simple if
\mathsf{P}_{k}(x-1)
.

Recall that in Section
3
we defined standard modules for every

\nu\vdash m
, where
0\leq m\leq k
,
\Delta_{k}(\nu)
with basis

\mathcal{B}_{k}(\nu)=\{d\otimes T\,|\,d\text{ a }(k,m)\text{-standard diagram and }T\text{ standard tableau of shape }\nu\}.

As in Section
3
, we will assume that
the algebra
\mathsf{P}_{k}(x)
is semisimple
[
MS
]
;
that is we assume that
x\notin\{0,1,2,\ldots,2k-2\}
.

Recall that
V(k,m)
contains diagrams
d
such that
p(d)=m
and the only propagating vertices in the bottom row are:
\overline{1},\ldots,\overline{m}
.

Lemma 5.1
.

For
d\otimes T\in\mathcal{B}_{k}(\nu)
, we have
\pi^{\otimes k}d\otimes T=0
if
d
has singleton vertices on the top row. And if
d
has no singleton vertex in the top row, then

\overline{d\otimes T}:=\pi^{\otimes k}d\otimes T=d\otimes T+\sum_{d^{\prime}\otimes T^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}_{k}(\nu)}a_{d^{\prime}}(x)d^{\prime}\otimes T^{\prime}~,

where
a_{d^{\prime}}(x)\in\mathbb{C}(x)
for some
0\leq i\leq k
. In addition,
d^{\prime}
has singletons in the first row.

Proof.

If
d
has isolated vertices on the top row then
\pi^{\otimes k}\cdot d=0
by Lemma
4.2
.
If
d
has no singleton vertices in the first row, we have that
since
\pi^{\otimes k}\in\mathsf{P}_{k}(x)
and
\Delta_{k}(\nu)
is a
\mathsf{P}_{k}(x)
-module,
the
\pi^{\otimes k}\cdot d\otimes T
is a linear combination of elements in

\mathcal{B}_{k}(\nu)
. The terms of
\pi^{\otimes k}\cdot d\otimes T

are of obtained from
\frac{1}{(-x)^{J}}\mathsf{p}_{J}d\otimes T
, where

\mathsf{p}_{J}d\in V(k,m)
or equal to zero if the propagation number of
the resulting diagram
d^{\prime}
is less than
m
. If the diagram
d^{\prime}
in the
product of
\mathsf{p}_{J}d
has propagation number
m
, then

d^{\prime}=d_{1}\sigma
for some
\sigma\in S_{m}
and
d_{1}
has the same
top as
d^{\prime}
and is a
(k,m)
-standard diagram.
∎

.

k=3

5.1
:

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\otimes\raisebox{-3.61371pt}{$\vbox{\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0pt\halign{&\vsquare{#}\cr\vbox{\hbox{\vrule width=0.4pt\vbox to14.0pt{\hrule height=0.4pt\vss\hbox to14.0pt{\hss$1$\hss}\vss\hrule height=0.4pt}
\vrule width=0.4pt}\kern-0.4pt}\kern-0.4pt\cr}}$}

We define
\mathcal{Q}_{k}(\nu)
to be the set of nonzero
\pi^{\otimes k}d\otimes T
, for
d\otimes T\in\mathcal{B}_{k}(\nu)
.

Proposition 5.3
.

Let
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, for each
\nu\vdash m
such that
0\leq m\leq k
the set
\mathcal{Q}_{k}(\nu)
is linearly independent. And its size is the number of
[k]
-set tableaux of shape
(n-|\nu|,\nu)
such that no sets of size one are in the first row of the tableau.

Proof.

The linear independence follows from Lemma
5.1
.
The leading elements in each nonzero
\pi^{\otimes k}d\otimes T

is in bijection with the tableaux described in the statement of the Lemma using the bijection,
\rho
, from Section
3
.
∎

Define
\mathsf{QP}_{k}^{\nu}
to be the
\mathbb{C}(x)
-Span of the
elements in
\mathcal{Q}_{k}(\nu)
for every
\nu\vdash m
and
0\leq m\leq k
.

Proposition 5.4
.

Let
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
. For every
\nu\vdash m
and
0\leq m\leq k
,

\mathsf{QP}_{k}^{\nu}
is a simple module of
\mathsf{QP}_{k}(x)
.

Proof.

It is straightforward to show that
\mathsf{QP}_{k}^{\nu}
is a module of
\mathsf{QP}_{k}(\nu)
.
Every basis element in
\mathsf{QP}_{k}^{\nu}
is of the form
\pi^{\otimes k}d^{\prime}\otimes T

for some
d^{\prime}
without singletons in the first row. Thus, if
\overline{d}

is a basis element in
\mathsf{QP}_{k}(x)
, then

\displaystyle\overline{d}\cdot\overline{d^{\prime}\otimes T}

\displaystyle=\pi^{\otimes k}d\pi^{\otimes k}\pi^{\otimes k}d^{\prime}\otimes T

\displaystyle=\pi^{\otimes k}d\pi^{\otimes k}d^{\prime}\otimes T

\displaystyle=\pi^{\otimes k}(d\cdot\overline{d^{\prime}\otimes T})

Notice that
d\cdot\overline{d^{\prime}\otimes T}
is a linear combination of elements in
\mathcal{B}_{k}(\nu)
; therefore,
\pi^{\otimes k}(d\cdot\overline{d^{\prime}\otimes T})
is a linear combination of elements in
\mathcal{Q}_{k}(\nu)
.

To show that
\mathsf{QP}_{k}^{\nu}
is simple
\mathsf{QP}_{k}(x)
-module, we will show that it is generated by every nonzero element.
We know that the standard module,
\Delta_{k}(\nu)
, is a simple
\mathsf{P}_{k}(x)
-module, then it is generated by every nonzero element.
If
\pi^{\otimes k}\cdot d\otimes T\neq 0
, then it generates
\Delta_{k}(\nu)
as a
\mathsf{P}_{k}(x)
-module.
Therefore, for every
d^{\prime}\otimes T^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}_{k}(\nu)
such that
d^{\prime}
has no
singletons in its top row, there must be an
f\in\mathsf{P}_{k}(x)
such that

f\cdot\pi^{\otimes k}d\otimes T=d^{\prime}\otimes T^{\prime}~,

Hence the element
\overline{f}=\pi^{\otimes k}f\pi^{\otimes k}
has the property that

\overline{f}\cdot\pi^{\otimes k}d\otimes T=\overline{d^{\prime}\otimes T^{\prime}}\in\mathcal{Q}_{k}(\nu).

Since
\mathsf{QP}_{k}^{\nu}
is the span of the elements
\overline{d^{\prime}\otimes T^{\prime}}
, by linearity we can generate all its elements by acting on the nonzero element
\pi^{\otimes k}d\otimes T
, and more generally on any nonzero linear combination of these elements.
∎

Recall that
\Delta_{k}(\nu)\cong V(k,m)\otimes_{S_{m}}\mathbb{S}^{\nu}
. Let
\pi^{\otimes k}V(k,m)=\{\pi^{\otimes k}d\,|\,d\in\widehat{\mathsf{D}}_{k}\}
, where in
\pi^{\otimes k}d
we set
\mathsf{p}_{J}d=0
, whenever
p(\mathsf{p}_{J}d)<m
due to the action of
\mathsf{P}_{k}(x)
on
V(k,m)
. Therefore, we have that

\mathsf{QP}_{k}^{\nu}\cong\pi^{\otimes k}V(k,m)\otimes_{S_{m}}\mathbb{S}^{\nu}~,

where
\mathbb{S}^{\nu}
are the simple modules of
\mathbb{S}^{m}
.

Theorem 5.5
.

Let
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
,
the set
\{\mathsf{QP}_{k}^{\nu}\,|\,\nu\vdash m\text{ where }0\leq m\leq k\}
forms a complete set of mutually non-isomorphic simple modules for
\mathsf{QP}_{k}(x)
.

Proof.

We have already shown in Proposition
5.4
that the
\mathsf{QP}_{k}^{\nu}
are simple modules. By Proposition
4.8
,
\mathsf{QP}_{k}(x)
contains a group isomorphic to
S_{k}
. For an element
\pi^{\otimes k}\sigma
and
\pi^{\otimes k}d\otimes T\in\pi^{\otimes k}V(k,m)\otimes_{S_{m}}\mathbb{S}^{\nu}
we have

\pi^{\otimes k}\sigma\cdot\pi^{\otimes k}d\otimes T=\pi^{\otimes k}\sigma d\otimes T=\pi^{\otimes k}d^{\prime}\otimes\tau T~,

where
\tau\in S_{m}
by Lemma
3.2
. Hence if
\mu\neq\nu
, then the group generated by
\overline{\mathsf{s}_{i}}
act differently on
\mathsf{QP}_{k}^{\nu}
than on
\mathsf{QP}_{k}^{\mu}
. Therefore, these modules are not isomorphic.
∎

We note that using the bijection
\rho
from Section
3

and setting
x=n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
with
n\geq 2k
, we can give
an equivalent description of simple
\mathsf{QP}_{k}(n)
-modules using
tableaux.
Extending
\rho
linearly
we map
\rho(\overline{d\otimes T})
to linear combination of tableaux.
In the following example we do this for
k=1
and
k=2
.

Example 5.6
.

For
k=1
, there are two simple modules of
\mathsf{P}_{1}(n)
